Emperor

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 4쪽 10줄 · 40줄 · 약 2:11
Many years ago there lived an Emperor who loved new clothes more than anything. <break time="0.8s" />
He spent all his money on fine clothes. <break time="0.8s" />
He did not care about his soldiers or about the theater. <break time="0.8s" />
He had a different coat for every hour of the day. <break time="0.8s" />
People usually said, The king is in his meeting room. <break time="0.8s" />
But about this Emperor they said, He is in his dressing room. <break time="0.8s" />
One day two swindlers came to the city. <break time="0.8s" />
They said that they were weavers of wonderful cloth. <break time="0.8s" />
Our cloth is magic, they said. <break time="0.8s" />
Foolish people cannot see it at all. <break time="0.8s" />
The Emperor was very excited and gave them a lot of gold. <break time="0.8s" />
The swindlers set up two looms in a room. <break time="0.8s" />
They pretended to work hard, but there was nothing on the looms. <break time="0.8s" />
They put the gold into their own bags. <break time="0.8s" />
After a while, the Emperor wanted to know how the cloth was coming along. <break time="0.8s" />
But he felt a little nervous about going himself. <break time="0.8s" />
So he sent his honest old minister to look. <break time="0.8s" />
The old minister opened his eyes wide, but he could not see anything. <break time="0.8s" />
Am I a fool, he thought, No one must know. <break time="0.8s" />
So he said, Oh, it is beautiful, I will tell the Emperor. <break time="0.8s" />
Soon the Emperor sent another official to check the cloth. <break time="0.8s" />
He also saw nothing, but he also praised the cloth. <break time="0.8s" />
What lovely colors, he said loudly. <break time="0.8s" />
At last the Emperor went to see the cloth himself. <break time="0.8s" />
He looked and looked, but the looms were empty. <break time="0.8s" />
How terrible, he thought, Am I not fit to be the Emperor. <break time="0.8s" />
But he smiled and said, Oh, it is very pretty, I like it very much. <break time="0.8s" />
All the people with him said the same thing. <break time="0.8s" />
You should wear these clothes in the big parade, they said. <break time="0.8s" />
The Emperor gave each swindler a medal and called them Royal Weavers. <break time="0.8s" />
The night before the parade, the swindlers stayed up all night. <break time="0.8s" />
They cut the air with big scissors and sewed with needles that had no thread. <break time="0.8s" />
At last they said, Now the Emperors new clothes are ready. <break time="0.8s" />
The Emperor took off his clothes, and the swindlers pretended to dress him. <break time="0.8s" />
Here are the pants, and here is the coat, they said. <break time="0.8s" />
They are as light as a spiders web. <break time="0.8s" />
You will feel as if you are wearing nothing at all. <break time="0.8s" />
The Emperor turned around and around in front of the mirror. <break time="0.8s" />
How well they fit, everyone cried. <break time="0.8s" />
Two servants bent down and pretended to lift the long train of his robe.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/25쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 11줄 · 11줄 · 약 0:37
The Emperor walked proudly under a golden umbrella in the parade. <break time="0.8s" />
All the people in the streets and at the windows cried out. <break time="0.8s" />
What beautiful new clothes, they said, How wonderful they look. <break time="0.8s" />
No one wanted others to think that he was a fool. <break time="0.8s" />
Then a little child said, But he has nothing on. <break time="0.8s" />
Listen to the innocent child, said his father. <break time="0.8s" />
One person whispered to another, and soon everyone was saying it. <break time="0.8s" />
He has nothing on, shouted all the people at last. <break time="0.8s" />
The Emperor shivered, because he knew they were right. <break time="0.8s" />
But he thought, I must go on with the parade. <break time="0.8s" />
So he walked even more proudly, and the servants kept holding the train that was not there.

HappyPrince

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 4쪽 8줄 · 41줄 · 약 2:28
High above the city, on a tall column, stood the statue of the Happy Prince. <break time="0.8s" />
He was covered all over with thin leaves of fine gold. <break time="0.8s" />
For eyes he had two bright sapphires, and a large red ruby glowed on his sword. <break time="0.8s" />
Everyone in the city admired him very much. <break time="0.8s" />
He is as beautiful as a weathercock, said one of the town leaders. <break time="0.8s" />
Why can you not be like the Happy Prince, a mother asked her crying little boy. <break time="0.8s" />
One night a little Swallow flew over the city. <break time="0.8s" />
His friends had gone away to Egypt six weeks before, but he had stayed behind. <break time="0.8s" />
He was looking for a place to sleep. <break time="0.8s" />
I will stay at the statue, he cried. <break time="0.8s" />
It is a fine place with plenty of fresh air. <break time="0.8s" />
So he landed just between the feet of the Happy Prince. <break time="0.8s" />
Just as he was putting his head under his wing, a large drop of water fell on him. <break time="0.8s" />
How strange, he said. <break time="0.8s" />
There is not a single cloud in the sky. <break time="0.8s" />
Then another drop fell, and he looked up. <break time="0.8s" />
The eyes of the Happy Prince were filled with tears. <break time="0.8s" />
Who are you, asked the Swallow. <break time="0.8s" />
I am the Happy Prince, said the statue. <break time="0.8s" />
Then why are you crying. <break time="0.8s" />
When I was alive, I lived in a palace where sadness was not allowed to enter, said the Prince. <break time="0.8s" />
Now that I am dead, they have set me up here so high that I can see all the misery of my city. <break time="0.8s" />
Though my heart is made of lead, I cannot help crying. <break time="0.8s" />
Far away in a little street there is a poor house, the statue went on. <break time="0.8s" />
A woman is sitting at a table, sewing flowers onto a satin gown for the Queens maid. <break time="0.8s" />
Her little boy is lying ill in the corner, and he is asking for oranges. <break time="0.8s" />
His mother has nothing to give him but river water, so he is crying. <break time="0.8s" />
Swallow, little Swallow, will you not bring her the ruby out of my sword. <break time="0.8s" />
The Swallow wanted to fly to Egypt, but the Prince looked so sad that he agreed. <break time="0.8s" />
He picked out the great ruby and flew away over the roofs of the town. <break time="0.8s" />
He laid the ruby on the table beside the womans thimble. <break time="0.8s" />
Then he flew gently round the bed, fanning the boys hot forehead with his wings. <break time="0.8s" />
How cool I feel, said the boy, and he sank into a happy sleep. <break time="0.8s" />
The next night the Prince asked the Swallow to stay one more night. <break time="0.8s" />
In a cold attic I can see a young writer who is too hungry to finish his play. <break time="0.8s" />
Pluck out one of my eyes and take it to him. <break time="0.8s" />
I cannot do that, said the Swallow, and he began to weep. <break time="0.8s" />
But he did as the Prince asked, and the young man smiled with joy. <break time="0.8s" />
The night after, the Swallow took the other sapphire to a little match girl who had dropped her matches in the street. <break time="0.8s" />
Now the Prince was blind. <break time="0.8s" />
I will stay with you always, said the Swallow, and he slept at the Princes feet.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/25쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 16줄 · 16줄 · 약 1:02
My body is covered with fine gold, said the Prince. <break time="0.8s" />
Take it off, leaf by leaf, and give it to my poor. <break time="0.8s" />
Leaf after leaf, the Swallow carried the gold to the poor, and the childrens faces grew rosy. <break time="0.8s" />
Then the snow came, and after the snow came the frost. <break time="0.8s" />
The poor little Swallow grew colder and colder, but he would not leave the Prince. <break time="0.8s" />
At last he knew that he was going to die. <break time="0.8s" />
Goodbye, dear Prince, he whispered, and he kissed the Prince and fell down dead at his feet. <break time="0.8s" />
At that moment a strange crack sounded inside the statue, as if something had broken. <break time="0.8s" />
The lead heart had snapped right in two. <break time="0.8s" />
The next morning the Mayor looked up at the statue and said, How shabby the Happy Prince looks. <break time="0.8s" />
So they pulled the statue down and melted it, but the broken lead heart would not melt. <break time="0.8s" />
They threw it on a dust heap where the dead Swallow was also lying. <break time="0.8s" />
Bring me the two most precious things in the city, said God to one of His Angels. <break time="0.8s" />
The Angel brought Him the lead heart and the dead bird. <break time="0.8s" />
You have rightly chosen, said God. <break time="0.8s" />
In my garden this little bird shall sing forever, and the Happy Prince shall live in my city of gold.

Marley was dead. <break time="0.8s" />
There is no doubt about that. <break time="0.8s" />
Jacob Marley had been Ebenezer Scrooges business partner for many years, and when Marley died, Scrooge was his only friend and the only person who came to his funeral. <break time="0.8s" />
Even on the day of the funeral, Scrooge did not stop working. <break time="0.8s" />
He made an excellent deal that very day. <break time="0.8s" />
Scrooge was a tight fisted man. <break time="0.8s" />
He squeezed, grabbed, and held on to every penny. <break time="0.8s" />
He was as hard and sharp as flint, and as secretive and lonely as an oyster.

TomSawyer

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 4쪽 10줄 · 41줄 · 약 2:22
Saturday morning came, and the whole summer world was bright and fresh. <break time="0.8s" />
There was a song in every heart, and the trees were full of flowers. <break time="0.8s" />
Tom came out onto the sidewalk with a bucket of whitewash and a long brush. <break time="0.8s" />
He looked at the fence, and all the joy left him. <break time="0.8s" />
It was thirty yards of board fence, nine feet high. <break time="0.8s" />
Life seemed empty to him, and living was a heavy burden. <break time="0.8s" />
Sighing, he dipped his brush and passed it along the top board. <break time="0.8s" />
Then he compared the small white line with the huge fence. <break time="0.8s" />
He sat down on a box, feeling hopeless. <break time="0.8s" />
Just then Jim came skipping out of the gate with a tin bucket. <break time="0.8s" />
He was going to bring water from the town pump. <break time="0.8s" />
Jim, I will get the water if you whitewash some, said Tom. <break time="0.8s" />
But Jim shook his head and ran away, afraid of Aunt Polly. <break time="0.8s" />
Tom began to think of all the fun he had planned for this day. <break time="0.8s" />
Soon the free boys would come by and laugh at him for having to work. <break time="0.8s" />
He looked in his pockets, but he had only some toys and pieces of junk. <break time="0.8s" />
It was not enough to buy even half an hour of freedom. <break time="0.8s" />
At this dark and hopeless moment, a great idea burst upon him. <break time="0.8s" />
It was nothing less than a great, magnificent idea. <break time="0.8s" />
He took up his brush and went quietly to work. <break time="0.8s" />
Soon Ben Rogers came along, the boy whose laughing Tom feared most. <break time="0.8s" />
He was eating an apple and pretending to be a big steamboat. <break time="0.8s" />
Ding dong dong, he called, turning in a slow circle like a boat. <break time="0.8s" />
Tom went on whitewashing and paid no attention to the steamboat. <break time="0.8s" />
Hi, old boy, you have to work, do you not, said Ben. <break time="0.8s" />
Tom looked at him as if he were surprised and asked, Why, it is you, Ben, What do you call work. <break time="0.8s" />
Why, is that not work, asked Ben. <break time="0.8s" />
Well, maybe it is, and maybe it is not, said Tom. <break time="0.8s" />
All I know is that it suits Tom Sawyer. <break time="0.8s" />
Does a boy get a chance to whitewash a fence every day. <break time="0.8s" />
Ben stopped eating his apple and watched Tom with new interest. <break time="0.8s" />
Tom swept his brush back and forth like an artist. <break time="0.8s" />
He stepped back to look at the effect and added a touch here and there. <break time="0.8s" />
Ben watched every move and became more and more interested. <break time="0.8s" />
Say, Tom, let me whitewash a little, he said at last. <break time="0.8s" />
No, Ben, I cannot, Aunt Polly is very particular about this fence. <break time="0.8s" />
Oh, please let me try, I will give you the core of my apple. <break time="0.8s" />
Well, no, Ben, I am afraid, said Tom. <break time="0.8s" />
I will give you all of it, cried Ben. <break time="0.8s" />
Tom gave up the brush with an unwilling face but a happy heart. <break time="0.8s" />
While Ben worked and sweated in the sun, Tom sat in the shade and ate the apple.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/25쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 10줄 · 10줄 · 약 0:38
Boys came by all afternoon, and each one stayed to whitewash. <break time="0.8s" />
When Ben was tired, Billy Fisher paid a kite for the next turn. <break time="0.8s" />
Then Johnny Miller bought in with a dead rat and a string to swing it with. <break time="0.8s" />
By the evening, Tom was rich with treasures. <break time="0.8s" />
He had marbles, a piece of blue glass, a key that opened nothing, and a one eyed kitten. <break time="0.8s" />
The fence had three coats of whitewash on it. <break time="0.8s" />
If he had not run out of whitewash, every boy in the village would have been bankrupt. <break time="0.8s" />
Tom had discovered a great law of human action without knowing it. <break time="0.8s" />
To make someone want a thing, you only need to make it hard to get. <break time="0.8s" />
Work is what a person must do, and play is what a person does not have to do.

GiftOfTheMagi

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 3쪽 12줄 · 35줄 · 약 2:11
One dollar and eighty seven cents. <break time="0.8s" />
That was all the money Della had, and sixty cents of it was in pennies. <break time="0.8s" />
She had saved those pennies one or two at a time by bargaining with the grocer and the butcher. <break time="0.8s" />
Three times Della counted the money, and three times it was one dollar and eighty seven cents. <break time="0.8s" />
And the next day would be Christmas. <break time="0.8s" />
There was clearly nothing to do but flop down on the shabby little couch and cry. <break time="0.8s" />
So Della did it. <break time="0.8s" />
Life, she thought, is made up of sobs, sniffles, and smiles, with sniffles in the lead. <break time="0.8s" />
Della and her husband, Jim, lived in a small furnished flat that cost eight dollars a week. <break time="0.8s" />
Not long ago Jim had earned thirty dollars a week, but now his pay had fallen to twenty. <break time="0.8s" />
Still, whenever Jim came home, Della ran to him and hugged him warmly. <break time="0.8s" />
That was a happy thing to see. <break time="0.8s" />
Della finished her cry and powdered her cheeks. <break time="0.8s" />
She stood by the window and looked out sadly at a gray cat walking along a gray fence in a gray backyard. <break time="0.8s" />
Tomorrow would be Christmas Day, and she had only one dollar and eighty seven cents to buy Jim a present. <break time="0.8s" />
She had been saving every penny she could for months, and this was the result. <break time="0.8s" />
Suddenly she turned from the window and stood before the mirror. <break time="0.8s" />
Her eyes were shining brilliantly, but her face had lost its color. <break time="0.8s" />
Quickly she pulled down her hair and let it fall to its full length. <break time="0.8s" />
The James Dillingham Youngs had two possessions in which they both took great pride. <break time="0.8s" />
One was Jims gold watch, which had belonged to his father and his grandfather. <break time="0.8s" />
The other was Dellas hair. <break time="0.8s" />
It fell about her, rippling and shining like a brown waterfall, and reached below her knee. <break time="0.8s" />
Della put up her hair again, nervously and quickly. <break time="0.8s" />
For a moment she stood still while a tear or two splashed on the worn red carpet. <break time="0.8s" />
Then she put on her old brown jacket and her old brown hat. <break time="0.8s" />
With the brilliant sparkle still in her eyes, she hurried out the door and down the stairs to the street. <break time="0.8s" />
She stopped at a shop with a sign that said, Madame Sofronie, Hair Goods of All Kinds. <break time="0.8s" />
Della ran up one flight of stairs and caught her breath. <break time="0.8s" />
Will you buy my hair, asked Della. <break time="0.8s" />
I buy hair, said Madame. <break time="0.8s" />
Take your hat off and lets have a look at it. <break time="0.8s" />
Down rippled the brown waterfall. <break time="0.8s" />
Twenty dollars, said Madame, lifting the mass with a practiced hand. <break time="0.8s" />
Give it to me quick, said Della.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/24쪽 1줄 – 6쪽 19줄 · 44줄 · 약 2:44
The next two hours flew by on rosy wings. <break time="0.8s" />
Della searched every store in town for Jims present. <break time="0.8s" />
At last she found it, and it surely had been made for Jim and no one else. <break time="0.8s" />
It was a platinum chain for his watch, simple and plain in design. <break time="0.8s" />
It was like Jim himself, quiet and valuable. <break time="0.8s" />
She paid twenty one dollars for it and hurried home with the eighty seven cents. <break time="0.8s" />
With that chain on his watch, Jim could check the time proudly in any company. <break time="0.8s" />
Grand as the watch was, he sometimes looked at it secretly because of the old leather strap he used instead of a chain. <break time="0.8s" />
When Della reached home, she got out her curling irons and went to work. <break time="0.8s" />
Within forty minutes her head was covered with tiny, close lying curls. <break time="0.8s" />
She looked at herself in the mirror long and carefully. <break time="0.8s" />
Jim will say I look like a schoolboy, she worried. <break time="0.8s" />
At seven oclock the coffee was made, and the frying pan was ready on the back of the stove. <break time="0.8s" />
Jim was never late. <break time="0.8s" />
Della held the chain in her hand and sat on the corner of the table near the door. <break time="0.8s" />
She heard his step on the stairs, and for a moment she turned white. <break time="0.8s" />
Please God, make him think I am still pretty, she whispered. <break time="0.8s" />
The door opened, and Jim stepped in and closed it. <break time="0.8s" />
He looked thin and very serious. <break time="0.8s" />
Poor fellow, he was only twenty two, and he needed a new overcoat and gloves. <break time="0.8s" />
Jim stopped inside the door, as still as a hunting dog that smells a bird. <break time="0.8s" />
His eyes were fixed upon Della, and there was an expression in them that she could not read. <break time="0.8s" />
Jim, darling, she cried, do not look at me that way. <break time="0.8s" />
I had my hair cut off and sold because I could not live through Christmas without giving you a present. <break time="0.8s" />
It will grow out again, and my hair grows awfully fast. <break time="0.8s" />
Jim drew a package from his overcoat pocket and threw it upon the table. <break time="0.8s" />
Do not make any mistake, Dell, he said. <break time="0.8s" />
No haircut could make me like my girl any less. <break time="0.8s" />
But open that package, and you will see why I was so shocked. <break time="0.8s" />
White fingers quickly tore at the string and paper. <break time="0.8s" />
Inside lay the Combs, the set of combs that Della had admired for a long time in a shop window on Broadway. <break time="0.8s" />
They were beautiful combs, made of pure tortoise shell, with jeweled edges. <break time="0.8s" />
But the hair that should have worn them was gone. <break time="0.8s" />
Della hugged them to her chest and smiled through her tears. <break time="0.8s" />
My hair grows so fast, Jim. <break time="0.8s" />
Then she held out the chain to him eagerly upon her open palm. <break time="0.8s" />
Is it not lovely, Jim, Give me your watch. <break time="0.8s" />
I want to see how the chain looks on it. <break time="0.8s" />
Instead of obeying, Jim sat down on the couch, put his hands under the back of his head, and smiled. <break time="0.8s" />
Dell, said he, I sold the watch to get the money to buy your combs. <break time="0.8s" />
The magi, as you know, were wise men who brought gifts to the Baby in the manger. <break time="0.8s" />
Here I have told you the story of two foolish children who sacrificed for each other the greatest treasures of their house. <break time="0.8s" />
But of all who give gifts, these two were the wisest. <break time="0.8s" />
They are the magi.

당신 빗을 살. <break time="0.8s" />
동방박사들은. <break time="0.8s" />
여러분도 알다시피. <break time="0.8s" />
지혜로운 사람들이었다. <break time="0.8s" />
선물을 가져온. <break time="0.8s" />
구유 속 아기에게. <break time="0.8s" />
여기서 나는 여러분에게 이야기를 들려주었다. <break time="0.8s" />
어리석은 두 아이의. <break time="0.8s" />

LastLeaf

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 4쪽 9줄 · 38줄 · 약 2:28
In a little part of New York called Greenwich Village, the streets run crazily and cross each other in strange ways. <break time="0.8s" />
Artists soon came to live there because the rooms were old and cheap. <break time="0.8s" />
At the top of a short brick building, Sue and Johnsy had their studio. <break time="0.8s" />
They had met in May at a cheap restaurant and found that their tastes in art were the same. <break time="0.8s" />
So they decided to share a studio. <break time="0.8s" />
In November a cold, unseen stranger, whom the doctors called Pneumonia, walked through the Village. <break time="0.8s" />
He touched one person here and another there with his icy fingers. <break time="0.8s" />
One of the people he touched was Johnsy. <break time="0.8s" />
She lay in her bed, hardly moving, and looked through the window at the brick wall of the next house. <break time="0.8s" />
One morning the busy doctor called Sue into the hall. <break time="0.8s" />
She has one chance in ten, he said, and that chance is for her to want to live. <break time="0.8s" />
She has made up her mind that she is not going to get well. <break time="0.8s" />
After the doctor left, Sue went into Johnsys room with her drawing board, whistling a cheerful tune. <break time="0.8s" />
Johnsy was lying with her eyes wide open, counting backward. <break time="0.8s" />
Twelve, she said, and a little later, eleven, and then ten and nine. <break time="0.8s" />
Sue looked out of the window and saw only an old ivy vine climbing up the brick wall. <break time="0.8s" />
Leaves on the ivy, Johnsy whispered. <break time="0.8s" />
When the last one falls, I must go too. <break time="0.8s" />
I have known that for three days. <break time="0.8s" />
Oh, I never heard of such nonsense, said Sue, but Johnsy only closed her eyes. <break time="0.8s" />
Old Behrman was a painter who lived on the ground floor below them. <break time="0.8s" />
He was past sixty and had a long gray beard. <break time="0.8s" />
For forty years he had painted, but he had never been successful. <break time="0.8s" />
He was always about to paint a masterpiece, but he had never yet begun it. <break time="0.8s" />
He drank too much and laughed at softness in anyone. <break time="0.8s" />
Yet he thought of himself as a guard to protect the two young artists upstairs. <break time="0.8s" />
When Sue told him about Johnsys idea, he shouted angrily at such foolishness. <break time="0.8s" />
That night a cold rain fell, mixed with snow, and a fierce wind blew all night long. <break time="0.8s" />
Sue was sure that the last leaf would be gone by morning. <break time="0.8s" />
In the morning Johnsy told Sue to pull up the shade. <break time="0.8s" />
After the beating rain and fierce wind, one ivy leaf still hung against the brick wall. <break time="0.8s" />
It was dark green near the stem and yellow at the edges. <break time="0.8s" />
The leaf stayed there all day, and it was still there through the second stormy night. <break time="0.8s" />
I have been a bad girl, Sudie, said Johnsy. <break time="0.8s" />
Something has made that last leaf stay there to show me how wicked I was. <break time="0.8s" />
It is wrong to want to die, she said, and she asked for some soup. <break time="0.8s" />
The next day the doctor said, She is out of danger, and you have won. <break time="0.8s" />
But there is sad news, too, he added, Old Behrman has pneumonia, and there is no hope for him.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/25쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 12줄 · 12줄 · 약 0:43
Old Behrman died in the hospital two days later. <break time="0.8s" />
That afternoon Sue came to the bed where Johnsy lay and put her arm around her. <break time="0.8s" />
I have something to tell you, she said. <break time="0.8s" />
On the first stormy night, the janitor found Mister Behrman in his room, helpless with pain. <break time="0.8s" />
His shoes and clothes were wet through and icy cold. <break time="0.8s" />
They could not imagine where he had been on such a terrible night. <break time="0.8s" />
Then they found a lantern, still lit, and a ladder that had been dragged from its place. <break time="0.8s" />
There were also some brushes and a palette with green and yellow colors mixed on it. <break time="0.8s" />
Look out of the window, dear, at the last ivy leaf on the wall. <break time="0.8s" />
Did you not wonder why it never moved when the wind blew. <break time="0.8s" />
Ah, darling, it is Behrmans masterpiece. <break time="0.8s" />
He painted it there the night the last leaf fell.

TellTaleHeart

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 4쪽 11줄 · 43줄 · 약 2:36
True, I am nervous, very nervous, but why will you say that I am mad. <break time="0.8s" />
My illness has made my senses sharper, not weaker. <break time="0.8s" />
Above all, my hearing became very sharp. <break time="0.8s" />
Listen, and see how calmly I can tell you the whole story. <break time="0.8s" />
It is impossible to say how the idea first entered my brain. <break time="0.8s" />
I loved the old man, and he had never done anything wrong to me. <break time="0.8s" />
I did not want his money. <break time="0.8s" />
I think it was his eye. <break time="0.8s" />
He had the eye of a vulture, pale blue, with a thin film over it. <break time="0.8s" />
Whenever it looked at me, my blood ran cold. <break time="0.8s" />
So, little by little, I made up my mind to get rid of the eye forever. <break time="0.8s" />
You think I am mad, but you should have seen how wisely I worked. <break time="0.8s" />
I was never kinder to the old man than during the whole week before I did it. <break time="0.8s" />
Every night, about midnight, I turned his door handle and opened it, oh, so gently. <break time="0.8s" />
Then I put in a dark lantern, all closed, so that no light shone out. <break time="0.8s" />
It took me a whole hour to put my head inside the opening. <break time="0.8s" />
Would a madman have been so careful. <break time="0.8s" />
Then I opened the lantern just a crack, and a single thin ray fell upon the vulture eye. <break time="0.8s" />
I did this for seven long nights, but the eye was always closed. <break time="0.8s" />
So I could not do the work, because it was not the old man who troubled me, but his Evil Eye. <break time="0.8s" />
And every morning I went boldly into his room and asked how he had slept. <break time="0.8s" />
On the eighth night, I was even more careful than usual. <break time="0.8s" />
A clocks minute hand moves faster than my hand did. <break time="0.8s" />
I almost laughed at the idea that he did not know my secret thoughts. <break time="0.8s" />
Suddenly my thumb slipped on the metal of the lantern. <break time="0.8s" />
The old man sat up in bed, crying out, Who is there. <break time="0.8s" />
I kept quite still and said nothing for a whole hour. <break time="0.8s" />
He was still sitting up in bed, listening, just as I had done night after night. <break time="0.8s" />
Then I heard a low groan, and I knew it was the groan of deadly fear. <break time="0.8s" />
I knew that sound well, because it had often come from my own heart at night. <break time="0.8s" />
At last I opened the lantern, and the ray fell straight upon the vulture eye. <break time="0.8s" />
It was wide open, and I grew furious as I looked at it. <break time="0.8s" />
Then a low, dull, quick sound reached my ears, like a watch wrapped in cotton. <break time="0.8s" />
I knew that sound too, it was the beating of the old mans heart. <break time="0.8s" />
It grew quicker and quicker, and louder and louder every moment. <break time="0.8s" />
The old mans terror must have been extreme. <break time="0.8s" />
A new fear seized me, the sound would be heard by a neighbor. <break time="0.8s" />
With a loud yell, I threw open the lantern and jumped into the room. <break time="0.8s" />
He cried once, only once, and then the room was silent. <break time="0.8s" />
The old man was dead, and his eye would trouble me no more. <break time="0.8s" />
Next, I worked quickly but silently to hide the body. <break time="0.8s" />
I took up three boards from the floor and placed him under them. <break time="0.8s" />
Then I put the boards back so cleverly that no human eye could have found anything wrong.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/25쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 13줄 · 13줄 · 약 0:53
It was four oclock in the morning when someone knocked at the street door. <break time="0.8s" />
Three police officers came in, very politely. <break time="0.8s" />
A neighbor had heard a scream during the night, and they had come to search the house. <break time="0.8s" />
I smiled, for what had I to fear. <break time="0.8s" />
I told them the scream was my own, in a dream. <break time="0.8s" />
I brought chairs into the old mans room and placed my own chair right above the boards. <break time="0.8s" />
The officers were satisfied, and we talked happily about ordinary things. <break time="0.8s" />
But soon I felt myself getting pale, and I wished them gone. <break time="0.8s" />
A ringing began in my ears, a low, dull, quick sound, much like a watch wrapped in cotton. <break time="0.8s" />
It grew louder and louder, but the officers chatted pleasantly and smiled. <break time="0.8s" />
Was it possible they did not hear it, No, no, They heard, and they were making fun of my terror. <break time="0.8s" />
I could bear those smiles no longer, and I felt that I must scream or die. <break time="0.8s" />
Villains, I shrieked, I admit the deed, Tear up the boards, Here, here, It is the beating of his terrible heart.

LadyOrTiger

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 4쪽 10줄 · 38줄 · 약 2:27
Long ago, there lived a king who was half barbaric. <break time="0.8s" />
His ideas were large and wild, and he had the power to turn them into facts. <break time="0.8s" />
When everything went as he wished, he was gentle and friendly. <break time="0.8s" />
But when something went wrong, he was even more gentle and friendly, for nothing pleased him more than making crooked things straight. <break time="0.8s" />
He built a huge public arena with seats all around it. <break time="0.8s" />
But the arena was not for sports or fights between men and wild animals. <break time="0.8s" />
It was a place where justice was decided by chance alone. <break time="0.8s" />
When a man was accused of a crime, his fate was decided in this arena. <break time="0.8s" />
The king believed that this system was perfectly fair. <break time="0.8s" />
The accused man stepped into the arena and faced two doors, exactly alike and side by side. <break time="0.8s" />
He had to open one of them, and no one could help him choose. <break time="0.8s" />
Behind one door was a hungry tiger, the fiercest that could be found. <break time="0.8s" />
If he opened that door, the tiger would leap upon him at once as punishment for his guilt. <break time="0.8s" />
Behind the other door was a lady, the most suitable one the king could find for him. <break time="0.8s" />
If he opened this door, he would marry her right there, as a reward for his innocence. <break time="0.8s" />
It did not matter whether he already had a wife or loved someone else. <break time="0.8s" />
The people loved these trials, because no one ever knew which result was coming. <break time="0.8s" />
They might see a terrible death or a happy wedding. <break time="0.8s" />
The king had a beautiful daughter, whose spirit was as fiery as his own. <break time="0.8s" />
He loved her more than anything in the world. <break time="0.8s" />
Among the kings servants was a young man of low birth. <break time="0.8s" />
He was handsome and brave, and the princess loved him deeply. <break time="0.8s" />
Her love was warm and strong, and it had a great deal of fire in it. <break time="0.8s" />
One day the king discovered their secret love. <break time="0.8s" />
Without a moments delay, he threw the young man into prison. <break time="0.8s" />
A day was set for his trial in the arena. <break time="0.8s" />
The fiercest tiger and the most beautiful young lady in the land were chosen for the doors. <break time="0.8s" />
Everyone knew that the young man had loved the princess. <break time="0.8s" />
The day came, and the arena was filled with thousands of people. <break time="0.8s" />
The young man walked in, tall and handsome, and bowed to the king. <break time="0.8s" />
But his eyes went straight to the princess, who sat at her fathers right side. <break time="0.8s" />
With gold and the power of her will, the princess had learned the secret of the doors. <break time="0.8s" />
She knew in which room the tiger waited and in which room the lady stood. <break time="0.8s" />
She also knew who the lady was, and she hated her. <break time="0.8s" />
She had seen that lady look at the young man with admiring eyes. <break time="0.8s" />
Sometimes she thought he had looked back at her, too. <break time="0.8s" />
Her jealous heart burned with hatred for that beautiful lady. <break time="0.8s" />
The young man looked at her, and he understood at once that she knew the secret.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/25쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 12줄 · 12줄 · 약 0:49
His eyes asked her the question, Which. <break time="0.8s" />
There was not a moment to lose. <break time="0.8s" />
The princess raised her right hand a little, and no one saw it except the young man. <break time="0.8s" />
Without a pause, he walked across the arena and opened the door on the right. <break time="0.8s" />
Now the point of the story is this, did the tiger come out of that door, or did the lady. <break time="0.8s" />
The more we think about it, the harder it is to answer. <break time="0.8s" />
Night after night, the princess had imagined him torn by the tiger. <break time="0.8s" />
But more often she had imagined him opening the other door and marrying the lady she hated. <break time="0.8s" />
She had seen his happy face and heard the wedding bells in her mind, and she had cried in pain. <break time="0.8s" />
Would it not be better for him to die at once and wait for her in a better world. <break time="0.8s" />
She had thought about it for days and nights, and she had made her decision. <break time="0.8s" />
I leave the question to you, the reader, which came out of the opened door, the lady, or the tiger.

반쯤 야만적인. <break time="0.8s" />
그의 생각은 크고 거칠었다. <break time="0.8s" />

그는 훨씬 더 온화하고 다정했다. <break time="0.8s" />
왜냐하면 무엇도 그를 더 기쁘게 하지 않았다. <break time="0.8s" />
굽은 것을 곧게 펴는 것보다. <break time="0.8s" />

사방에 좌석이 있는. <break time="0.8s" />

Necklace

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 3쪽 10줄 · 28줄 · 약 2:16
She was one of those pretty and charming girls who are sometimes born, as if by a mistake of fate, into a family of clerks. <break time="0.8s" />
She had no dowry, no hopes, and no way of being known and loved by a rich and important man. <break time="0.8s" />
So she let herself be married to a minor clerk in the Ministry of Education. <break time="0.8s" />
She suffered endlessly, feeling that she had been born for every luxury and delicacy. <break time="0.8s" />
She suffered from the poorness of her home, from its worn walls, its ugly chairs, and its faded curtains. <break time="0.8s" />
When she sat down to dinner and her husband cried happily, Ah, good soup, she dreamed of silver dishes and elegant dinners. <break time="0.8s" />
She had no fine dresses, no jewels, nothing, and yet she loved nothing else. <break time="0.8s" />
She had a rich friend, a former schoolmate, whom she no longer wanted to visit. <break time="0.8s" />
She suffered so much when she came home from her visits that she cried for days. <break time="0.8s" />
One evening her husband came home with a proud look and handed her a large envelope. <break time="0.8s" />
It was an invitation to a grand ball at the Ministry, which he had worked very hard to get. <break time="0.8s" />
Instead of being delighted, she threw the invitation on the table and asked, What do you want me to do with this. <break time="0.8s" />
I have nothing to wear, she said, and two tears ran slowly down her cheeks. <break time="0.8s" />
Her husband had saved four hundred francs to buy a gun for hunting with his friends that summer. <break time="0.8s" />
He turned a little pale, but he gave her the money for a beautiful dress. <break time="0.8s" />
As the day of the ball came closer, Madame Loisel seemed sad and anxious again. <break time="0.8s" />
I do not have a single jewel to wear, she said, I will look like a poor woman. <break time="0.8s" />
Her husband cried, Go and see your friend Madame Forestier, and ask her to lend you some jewels. <break time="0.8s" />
Madame Forestier went to her wardrobe, took out a large jewel box, and said, Choose, my dear. <break time="0.8s" />
Suddenly, in a black satin box, she found a superb diamond necklace, and her heart began to beat with desire. <break time="0.8s" />
Can you lend me this, only this, she asked, and her friend said, Why, yes, of course. <break time="0.8s" />
At the ball Madame Loisel was a great success, for she was prettier than all the other women. <break time="0.8s" />
All the men looked at her and asked her name, and even the Minister noticed her. <break time="0.8s" />
She danced wildly, drunk with pleasure, in a cloud of happiness made of all this admiration. <break time="0.8s" />
They left at about four in the morning, and her husband put around her shoulders the simple coat he had brought. <break time="0.8s" />
Its poorness clashed with the elegance of her ball dress, so she hurried away to avoid being seen by the rich women. <break time="0.8s" />
At home she took off her coat before the mirror to see herself in all her glory once more. <break time="0.8s" />
But suddenly she gave a cry, for the necklace was no longer around her neck.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/24쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 14줄 · 24줄 · 약 1:57
They searched everywhere, in the folds of her dress, in the folds of her coat, and in the pockets, but they found nothing. <break time="0.8s" />
Her husband went out again and walked over the whole route they had taken home. <break time="0.8s" />
He went to the police and to the cab companies, and he even put an advertisement in the newspapers. <break time="0.8s" />
At the end of a week they had lost all hope, and Loisel, who looked five years older, said, We must replace the necklace. <break time="0.8s" />
In a shop at the Palais Royal, they found a string of diamonds that seemed exactly like the one they had lost. <break time="0.8s" />
It cost forty thousand francs, but they could have it for thirty six thousand. <break time="0.8s" />
Loisel had eighteen thousand francs from his father, and he borrowed the rest from anyone who would lend it. <break time="0.8s" />
He signed promises to pay, made ruinous agreements, and risked the rest of his life. <break time="0.8s" />
When Madame Loisel took the necklace back, Madame Forestier said coldly, You should have returned it sooner. <break time="0.8s" />
She did not open the box, and Madame Loisel felt a great relief. <break time="0.8s" />
Madame Loisel came to know the terrible life of the very poor, but she took her part bravely. <break time="0.8s" />
They sent away the maid and moved into a little room under the roof. <break time="0.8s" />
She washed the dishes, scrubbed the floors, and carried water up the stairs, stopping at every landing to breathe. <break time="0.8s" />
At the end of ten years they had paid everything back, with the interest and all. <break time="0.8s" />
Madame Loisel looked old now, with rough red hands, untidy hair, and a loud voice. <break time="0.8s" />
Sometimes, when her husband was at the office, she sat down by the window and thought of that evening long ago. <break time="0.8s" />
What would have happened if she had not lost that necklace, Who knows. <break time="0.8s" />
How strange life is, and how little is needed to ruin us or to save us. <break time="0.8s" />
One Sunday, walking on the Champs Élysées, she suddenly saw Madame Forestier, still young, still beautiful, still charming. <break time="0.8s" />
Now that everything was paid, she decided to tell her friend the whole story. <break time="0.8s" />
The friend did not recognize her at first and cried, Oh, my poor Mathilde, how you have changed. <break time="0.8s" />
Yes, I have had hard days since I saw you, and all because of you, Mathilde answered. <break time="0.8s" />
When she explained everything, Madame Forestier stopped and took both her hands. <break time="0.8s" />
Oh, my poor Mathilde, But mine was false, It was worth at most five hundred francs.

TheBet

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 3쪽 10줄 · 30줄 · 약 2:11
It was a dark autumn night, and the old banker was walking up and down his study. <break time="0.8s" />
He was remembering a party he had given fifteen years before. <break time="0.8s" />
Many clever people had been there, and they had talked about the death penalty. <break time="0.8s" />
Most of the guests thought that the death penalty was wrong and should be replaced by life in prison. <break time="0.8s" />
I disagree, said the banker, The death penalty is kinder than life in prison. <break time="0.8s" />
An execution kills a man at once, but life in prison kills him slowly. <break time="0.8s" />
Which is more humane, to kill you in a few minutes or to take your life away over many years. <break time="0.8s" />
Among the guests was a young lawyer of about twenty five. <break time="0.8s" />
Both are wrong, he said, but if I had to choose, I would choose life in prison. <break time="0.8s" />
To live in any way is better than not to live at all. <break time="0.8s" />
That is not true, shouted the banker, I bet you two million you could not stay in a prison cell for even five years. <break time="0.8s" />
If you mean it seriously, said the lawyer, I will stay not five, but fifteen years. <break time="0.8s" />
The bet was agreed, and the banker, who had more millions than he could count, was delighted. <break time="0.8s" />
Later that evening, the banker began to think more seriously. <break time="0.8s" />
What is the point of this bet, What will it prove. <break time="0.8s" />
For me it was the foolishness of a rich man, and for him it was simple greed for money. <break time="0.8s" />
It was decided that the lawyer would live alone in a small building in the bankers garden. <break time="0.8s" />
He could not see any people, hear human voices, or receive letters or newspapers. <break time="0.8s" />
But he could have books, music, and wine, and he could ask for them by writing notes through a small window. <break time="0.8s" />
If he left even two minutes early, the banker would not have to pay him anything. <break time="0.8s" />
In the first year, the prisoner suffered terribly from loneliness and boredom. <break time="0.8s" />
Day and night the piano could be heard from his room, and he read only light, easy novels. <break time="0.8s" />
In the second year, the music stopped, and he asked only for the great classics. <break time="0.8s" />
In the sixth year, he began studying languages, philosophy, and history with great passion. <break time="0.8s" />
The banker hardly had time to order the books he asked for. <break time="0.8s" />
In four years, about six hundred books were sent to him. <break time="0.8s" />
Later he wrote that he had learned six languages and that his soul was filled with joy. <break time="0.8s" />
After that, he read the Bible and books about religion for a long time. <break time="0.8s" />
In the last two years, he read everything without any order, science one day and poetry the next. <break time="0.8s" />
He read like a drowning man who grabs at one piece of wood after another.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/24쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 12줄 · 22줄 · 약 1:42
Now the old banker remembered all this and thought, Tomorrow at twelve oclock he will be free. <break time="0.8s" />
If I pay him two million, I will be completely ruined. <break time="0.8s" />
Fifteen years before, he had more money than he could count, but now he was afraid to ask himself which he had more of, money or debts. <break time="0.8s" />
Reckless gambling on the stock market had slowly destroyed his fortune. <break time="0.8s" />
The only way to save myself from shame is for that man to die, he whispered. <break time="0.8s" />
At three oclock in the morning, he took the key to the lodge and went quietly into the garden. <break time="0.8s" />
It was dark and cold, and the watchman was asleep somewhere because of the bad weather. <break time="0.8s" />
Through the window, he saw the prisoner sitting at the table, not moving at all. <break time="0.8s" />
He was only skin and bones, with long hair like a womans and a wild, shaggy beard. <break time="0.8s" />
His head was resting on his hands, and in front of him lay a sheet of paper covered with small writing. <break time="0.8s" />
The banker took the paper from the table and read it. <break time="0.8s" />
Tomorrow at twelve oclock I will be free, but before I leave this room, I must say a few words to you. <break time="0.8s" />
For fifteen years I have studied life on earth through books. <break time="0.8s" />
In your books I have climbed mountains, loved beautiful women, and spoken with great thinkers. <break time="0.8s" />
And now I despise all your books, and I despise all the wisdom and blessings of this world. <break time="0.8s" />
Everything is empty and false, like a dream, You may be proud and wise and beautiful, but death will wipe you off the earth. <break time="0.8s" />
To show you how much I despise what you live for, I give up the two million. <break time="0.8s" />
I will leave here five hours before the time and so break our agreement. <break time="0.8s" />
The banker put the paper back on the table, kissed the strange man on the head, and went out of the lodge, weeping. <break time="0.8s" />
Never before had he felt such deep contempt for himself. <break time="0.8s" />
The next morning, the watchman ran in and reported that the prisoner had climbed out of the window and disappeared. <break time="0.8s" />
The banker went to the lodge, took the paper, and locked it in his safe.

Araby

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/21쪽 1줄 – 3쪽 8줄 · 24줄 · 약 2:33
North Richmond Street, being a dead end, was a quiet street except at the hour when the Christian Brothers School set the boys free. <break time="0.8s" />
At the blind end stood an uninhabited house of two storeys, detached from its neighbours on a square ground. <break time="0.8s" />
The other houses of the street, conscious of decent lives within them, gazed at one another with brown, imperturbable faces. <break time="0.8s" />
The former tenant of our house, a priest, had died in the back drawing room, and the air, musty from having been long enclosed, hung in all the rooms. <break time="0.8s" />
Among the useless papers in the waste room behind the kitchen, I found a few old books whose pages were curled and damp. <break time="0.8s" />
He had been a very charitable priest, for in his will he had left all his money to institutions and the furniture of his house to his sister. <break time="0.8s" />
When the short days of winter came, dusk fell before we had well eaten our dinners, and we played in the dark, muddy lanes behind the houses. <break time="0.8s" />
If Mangans sister came out on the doorstep to call her brother in to his tea, we watched her from our shadow peering up and down the street. <break time="0.8s" />
Every morning I lay on the floor in the front parlour watching her door, keeping the blind pulled down to within an inch of the sash so that I could not be seen. <break time="0.8s" />
When she came out on the doorstep, my heart leaped, and I ran to the hall, seized my books, and followed her, always keeping her brown figure in my eye. <break time="0.8s" />
I had never spoken to her, except for a few casual words, and yet her name was like a summons to all my foolish blood. <break time="0.8s" />
Her image accompanied me even in places the most hostile to romance, such as the crowded market where my aunt went shopping on Saturday evenings. <break time="0.8s" />
We walked through the flaring streets, jostled by drunken men and bargaining women, amid the curses of labourers and the shrill cries of shop boys. <break time="0.8s" />
These noises converged in a single sensation of life for me, and I imagined that I bore my chalice safely through a throng of foes. <break time="0.8s" />
One evening I went into the back drawing room in which the priest had died, and it was a dark, rainy evening, and there was no sound in the house. <break time="0.8s" />
Pressing the palms of my hands together until they trembled, I murmured, O love, O love, many times. <break time="0.8s" />
At last she spoke to me, and when she addressed the first words to me, I was so confused that I did not know what to answer. <break time="0.8s" />
She asked me whether I was going to Araby, and she said that it would be a splendid bazaar and that she would love to go. <break time="0.8s" />
She could not go, however, because there would be a retreat that week in her convent school. <break time="0.8s" />
While she spoke, she turned a silver bracelet round and round her wrist, and the light from the lamp caught the white curve of her neck. <break time="0.8s" />
It is well for you, she said, and I answered, If I go, I will bring you something. <break time="0.8s" />
What innumerable follies laid waste my waking and sleeping thoughts after that evening. <break time="0.8s" />
I wished to annihilate the tedious days in between, and the serious work of life seemed to me ugly, monotonous childs play. <break time="0.8s" />
At school I could not concentrate, and the syllables of the word Araby were called to me through the silence, casting an Eastern enchantment over me.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/24쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 7줄 · 15줄 · 약 1:35
On Saturday morning I reminded my uncle that I wished to go to the bazaar in the evening, and he answered curtly, Yes, boy, I know. <break time="0.8s" />
At seven oclock the servant came to tell me that my uncle had not yet come home, and I sat staring at the clock for some time. <break time="0.8s" />
When its ticking began to irritate me, I left the room and walked up and down the cold, empty, gloomy rooms upstairs, singing to myself. <break time="0.8s" />
At nine oclock I heard my uncles latchkey in the hall door, and I could tell from the way he talked to himself that he had been drinking. <break time="0.8s" />
When I asked him a second time for the money to go to the bazaar, he said that he had forgotten all about it. <break time="0.8s" />
Finally he gave me a florin, and I held it tightly in my hand as I walked quickly down to the station. <break time="0.8s" />
I took my seat in a third class carriage of a deserted train, which, after an intolerable delay, crept slowly out of the station. <break time="0.8s" />
It was ten minutes to ten when I finally reached the bazaar, and I saw the large building with its magical name shining above the door. <break time="0.8s" />
Nearly all the stalls were closed, and the greater part of the hall was in darkness, and I recognised a silence like that which pervades a church after a service. <break time="0.8s" />
At the door of one stall, a young lady was talking and laughing with two young gentlemen, and I noticed their English accents. <break time="0.8s" />
O, I never said such a thing, she said, and their silly, flirting talk went on as if I were not there. <break time="0.8s" />
Observing me, the young lady came over and asked me whether I wished to buy anything, but her tone was not encouraging. <break time="0.8s" />
No, thank you, I said, and I stood there for a while, pretending to be interested in her wares, knowing that my staying was useless. <break time="0.8s" />
Then I heard a voice at one end of the gallery calling that the light was out, and the upper part of the hall was now completely dark. <break time="0.8s" />
Gazing up into the darkness, I saw myself as a creature driven and derided by vanity, and my eyes burned with anguish and anger.

Heidegger

영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 1/31쪽 1줄 – 2쪽 8줄 · 16줄 · 약 1:55
That very singular man, old Doctor Heidegger, once invited four venerable friends to meet him in his study, where he intended to conduct a peculiar experiment. <break time="0.8s" />
There were three white bearded gentlemen, Mister Medbourne, Colonel Killigrew, and Mister Gascoigne, and a withered lady whose name was the Widow Wycherly. <break time="0.8s" />
Mister Medbourne, in the vigor of his age, had been a prosperous merchant, but he had lost everything through a frantic speculation and was now little better than a beggar. <break time="0.8s" />
Colonel Killigrew had wasted his best years, his health, and his fortune in the pursuit of sinful pleasures, which had left him with a body tormented by gout. <break time="0.8s" />
Mister Gascoigne was a ruined politician, a man of evil fame, who had been so completely forgotten that the present generation scarcely knew his name. <break time="0.8s" />
As for the Widow Wycherly, she had been a great beauty in her day, but scandalous stories had driven her from society, and she now lived in deep seclusion. <break time="0.8s" />
It is worth mentioning that each of the three old gentlemen had once been on the point of cutting the others throats for her sake. <break time="0.8s" />
Before going further, we must also say that Doctor Heidegger and all his guests were commonly thought to be a little out of their minds. <break time="0.8s" />
The study was a dim, old fashioned chamber, festooned with cobwebs and sprinkled with antique dust, where tall bookcases of oak lined the walls. <break time="0.8s" />
Over the central bookcase stood a bronze bust of Hippocrates, with which, according to some authorities, Doctor Heidegger was accustomed to consult in all difficult cases. <break time="0.8s" />
In the darkest corner hung a tall, narrow mirror, within which, it was fabled, the spirits of all the doctors dead patients dwelt. <break time="0.8s" />
The opposite side of the room was ornamented with the portrait of a young lady who, many years before, had been on the point of marrying Doctor Heidegger. <break time="0.8s" />
But, being affected with some slight disorder, she had swallowed one of her lovers prescriptions and died on the bridal evening. <break time="0.8s" />
The greatest curiosity of the study was a ponderous book, bound in black leather, which was well known to be a book of magic. <break time="0.8s" />
On the summer afternoon of our tale, a round table stood in the middle of the room, and on it was placed a cut glass vase of beautiful form. <break time="0.8s" />
The sunshine came through the window and fell upon the vase, so that a mild splendor was reflected from it onto the ashen faces of the five old people.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 2/33쪽 1줄 – 4쪽 8줄 · 17줄 · 약 2:12
Doctor Heidegger took from the black book a withered rose, whose green leaves and crimson petals had assumed one brownish hue, so that it seemed ready to crumble to dust. <break time="0.8s" />
This rose, said the doctor with a sigh, was given to me fifty five years ago by Sylvia Ward, whose portrait hangs yonder, and I meant to wear it at our wedding. <break time="0.8s" />
He then threw the faded flower into the water of the vase, and, to the astonishment of all, its crushed petals began to stir and to deepen in color. <break time="0.8s" />
Did you never hear of the Fountain of Youth, asked Doctor Heidegger, which Ponce de León, the Spanish adventurer, went in search of two or three centuries ago. <break time="0.8s" />
That fountain is situated in the southern part of Florida, and a friend of mine, knowing my curiosity in such matters, has sent me what you see in the vase. <break time="0.8s" />
The guests, though they did not believe a word of it, agreed to drink, for they thought that the experiment would be harmless at worst. <break time="0.8s" />
Before you drink, my respectable old friends, said the doctor, it would be well that you should draw up a few general rules for your guidance in passing a second time through the perils of youth. <break time="0.8s" />
Think what a sin and shame it would be if, with your peculiar advantages, you should not become patterns of virtue and wisdom to all the young people of the age. <break time="0.8s" />
The doctors four venerable friends made him no answer except a feeble and tremulous laugh, so ridiculous was the idea that they would ever go astray again. <break time="0.8s" />
Scarcely had they drunk the water when a sudden glow of cheerful sunshine seemed to brighten their faces, and the deep furrows of age began to fade away. <break time="0.8s" />
Give us more of this wondrous water, they cried eagerly, and with every glass the years seemed to roll back like a dream from which they were waking. <break time="0.8s" />
Mister Gascoignes mind seemed to run on political topics, and he talked in a deep, important voice about patriotism and national glory, though nobody could tell what he really meant. <break time="0.8s" />
Colonel Killigrew began to sing a merry drinking song, while Mister Medbourne was busy calculating a plan for supplying the East Indies with ice by harnessing whales to the polar icebergs. <break time="0.8s" />
As for the Widow Wycherly, she stood before the mirror, curtsying and simpering to her own image, and greeting it as the friend she loved better than all the world. <break time="0.8s" />
Soon the three gentlemen began to compete for her favor, each trying to snatch her hand, and their old rivalry burst into flame as if not a single day had passed. <break time="0.8s" />
In the struggle, the table was overturned, and the precious vase was dashed into a thousand fragments, while the Water of Youth flowed in a bright stream across the floor. <break time="0.8s" />
A butterfly, which had grown old and was ready to die, alighted on the snowy head of Doctor Heidegger after it had been touched by a few drops.
영어 전체 녹음 (문장) · Part 3/35쪽 1줄 – 5쪽 9줄 · 9줄 · 약 0:55
A strange chill, whether it was the chill of the room or of their own bodies, now began to creep gradually over them all. <break time="0.8s" />
The doctor took up the rose of half a century, and they saw with dismay that its fresh petals were already shriveling until it became as dry and fragile as before. <break time="0.8s" />
Looking at one another, they shuddered, for they saw that the deep furrows of age were returning and that each of them was once again an old, withered person. <break time="0.8s" />
Are we grown old again, so soon, cried they, dolefully, and indeed the Water of Youth possessed merely a virtue more transient than that of wine. <break time="0.8s" />
Yes, friends, ye are old again, said Doctor Heidegger, and, lo, the Water of Youth is all lavished on the ground, but I do not lament it. <break time="0.8s" />
For if the fountain gushed at my very doorstep, I would not stoop to bathe my lips in it, no, though its delirium were for years instead of moments. <break time="0.8s" />
Such is the lesson ye have taught me. <break time="0.8s" />
But the doctors four friends had taught no such lesson to themselves. <break time="0.8s" />
They resolved forthwith to make a pilgrimage to Florida, and there to drink at morning, noon, and night from the Fountain of Youth.
